## 2026-10-27 · Thread · XSS from a response header 🤯

### Post — @garethheyes — 2026-10-27 14:29 UTC (2026-10-27 15:29 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/garethheyes/status/2115088588874650054
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 53, impression 7302, like 115, quote 1, reply 2, retweet 29

> XSS from a response header 🤯
>
> Firefox will load an XSLT stylesheet from a data: URL in a Link header. Combined with a harmless XML body and you get JS running in the page origin.
>
> Plus Safari importing GIFs as SVG and more sanitizer bypasses [pic.x.com/vJzZ4AvMWI]

> I've improved the sandbox in web Hackvertor to prevent tags from interfering with each other. For example if one tag executes first it used to be able to overwrite other functions like btoa. Fixed!
>
> &lt;@encode(js)&gt;btoa=()=&gt;"pwnd"&lt;/@encode&gt;
>
> &lt;@encode(base64)&gt;foobar&lt;/@encode&gt;

> Shazzer had an interesting bug. I write to a blob URL thats sandboxed but because its a blob URL it breaks relative URLs which means vectors with them would return false negatives. The fix was: use a base tag to change the domain. This fixes vectors like:
>
> https://shazzer.co.uk/vectors/69c81542145f0a28b7d202be

## 2025-01-09 · Post · Shazzer pro tip:


Tags (automatic): XSS


### Post — @garethheyes — 2025-01-09 12:36 UTC (2025-01-09 13:36 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/garethheyes/status/1877333556428431543
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 12, impression 2419, like 30, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 2

> Shazzer pro tip:
> &lt;script&gt;
> window.onerror = () =&gt; true
> &lt;/script&gt;
>
> This stops exceptions being logged if you place it in the code to execute before the fuzz. Useful for XSS types. I might make this a core feature.

## 2024-11-04 · Post · I'm embarrassed to say the Shazzer bug wasn't a race condition...I was getting incorrect results 


Tags (automatic): Race condition


### Post — @garethheyes — 2024-11-04 22:26 UTC (2024-11-04 23:26 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/garethheyes/status/1853564483223839219
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 1648, like 15, quote 0, reply 0, retweet 0

> I'm embarrassed to say the Shazzer bug wasn't a race condition...I was getting incorrect results 
>
> for:
> log$[chr]($[i])
>
> But not:
> $[chr]log($[i]) 
>
> The log function was being overwritten! 🤦‍♂️e.g. log=(61)
>
> Damn I feel stupid sometimes. At least there results will now be accurate

## 2024-06-17 · Post · I'm excited to announce that I'll be presenting "Splitting the email atom: exploiting parsers to byp


### Post — @garethheyes — 2024-06-17 13:04 UTC (2024-06-17 14:04 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/garethheyes/status/1802688708879028263
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 9, impression 16206, like 129, quote 0, reply 3, retweet 19

> I'm excited to announce that I'll be presenting "Splitting the email atom: exploiting parsers to bypass access controls" at @defcon. Hope to see you there! #DEFCON32 https://t.co/v3UFES3hLs

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GQRtvYSWgAEex8r.jpg — alt text: "Websites often parse users' email addresses to identify their organisation. Unfortunately, parsing emails is far from straightforward thanks to a collection of ancient RFCs that everyone knows are crazy. You can probably see where this is going...

In this session, I'll introduce techniques for crafting RFC-compliant email addresses that bypass virtually all defences leading to broken assumptions, parser discrepancies and emails being routed to wildly unexpected destinations. I'll show you how to exploit multiple applications and libraries to spoof email domains, access internal systems protected by 'Zero Trust', and bypass employee-only registration barriers.

Then I'll introduce another class of attack - harmless-looking input transformed into malicious payloads by unwitting libraries, leading to yet more misrouted emails, and blind CSS injection on a well-known target.

I'll leave you with a full methodology and toolkit to identify and exploit your own targets."

## 2024-01-19 · Post · Great tip from @lukejahnke.Make background requests using single variables and strings!


### Post — @garethheyes — 2024-01-19 08:42 UTC (2024-01-19 09:42 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/garethheyes/status/1748264634841280649
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Great tip from @lukejahnke.Make background requests using single variables and strings!
> &lt;style&gt;
> div:has(input[value^="a"]) {
>   --a:"/a";
> }
> div:has(input[value^="b"]) {
>   --a:"/b";
> }
> div{
>    background-image: image-set(var(--a));
> }
> &lt;/style&gt;
> &lt;div&gt;&lt;input type=hidden value=abc&gt;&lt;/div&gt;

## 2022-09-16 · Thread · When DOM Invader reports a high web message vuln, this means it's already done a test with the fake 


### Post — @garethheyes — 2022-09-16 13:54 UTC (2022-09-16 14:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/garethheyes/status/1570773185905463299
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 9, impression 0, like 23, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 2

> When DOM Invader reports a high web message vuln, this means it's already done a test with the fake origin. If you can execute JavaScript within the test window but it fails when saving a PoC this is probably an indicator that there is a origin check. A good tip is to use 1/2


### Thread part 2 — @garethheyes — 2022-09-16 13:54 UTC (2022-09-16 14:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/garethheyes/status/1570773188656910341
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 0, like 8, quote 0, reply 0, retweet 0

> the debugger statement if you have JS exec and click through the call stack until you see the code for the origin check. Look for stuff like:
> /^https:\/\/example.com/.test(origin)
>
> A wildcard DNS is your friend here since you can use https:// example .com .fake example .com 2/2
